\title{Improving the displayed exponents in arXiv:2610.06783v1}
\author{A working derivation for review}
\date{October 15, 2026}
\begin{document}
\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
We sharpen the binomial-tail analysis of the paper's box data structure and
modify its encoding algorithm to omit unusable leaves. Under the same
assumptions $N\ge D^{18}$ and $|W|\le N^2/\sqrt D$, this gives deterministic
time $O(N^2/D^{0.07195})$, improving the displayed $0.063$ saving; with
Majdoddin's five-term tensor the same regime gives $O(N^2/D^{0.0991})$
preprocessing and $O(D^{0.4009})$ queries.
A separate reduction intersects independent modular filters before scanning
candidate witness blocks, and folds the residue classes of each modulus into
the outer dimensions of a single thin product per block, so that the band
encodings are shared across residues. It gives a Las Vegas algorithm for
integer all-edges Exact Triangle, min-plus product, and APSP in expected
$O(n^{2.995943})$ time; batching the independent middle-block products into
the tensor slots of one tile, with prefix-contracted queries, improves this
to $O(n^{2.995535})$. A bucket-pair version of the same folding reduces
integer 3SUM directly to one thin product per modulus and keeps the full
saving, giving expected $O(n^{1.996001})$ time. A deterministic sequence of
conditional modular moments gives deterministic integer 3SUM in
$O(n^{1.996200})$; applied to each outer pair, it gives deterministic integer all-edges Exact
Triangle, min-plus product, and APSP in $O(n^{2.996261})$. Combining this
with batching, deterministic cyclic reconstruction of omitted encodings,
and the SODA 2024 rectangular bounds improves the exponent to
$2.9958892$. A separate conditional statement, assuming the rectangular theorem of the
unrefereed preprint arXiv:2610.10494v2, whose stated bound at $k=1$ would
give $\omega\le2.25$, would give $2.9958323$; it is not counted among the
bounds derived here. A separate construction using
Majdoddin's five-term embedding and composite incidence certificates,
with fixed-profile inner-basis encodings and balanced prefix queries, gives deterministic
$O(n^{2.993676})$ time for integer all-edges Exact Triangle, min-plus product,
and APSP; used as a black box in the real-input reduction, the same data
structure gives real 3SUM and APSP expected times $O(n^{1.993805})$ and
$O(n^{2.993805})$. Splitting each composite modulus between residue folding and a
cyclic polynomial ring, and charging residual and stored-box queries
separately, further improves this deterministic bound to $O(n^{2.993441})$.
A partial cache of direct inner projections, partitioning each residual
query by its first cached positions, improves it to $O(n^{2.993401})$.
Changing the local identity, re-embedding the inputs with Walsh transforms,
and caching the remaining residual terms as polynomials gives
$O(n^{2.99294792})$ deterministic time. Deterministic middle translations
then restrict the rare source symbols; grouped completion across middle
blocks, asymmetric rectangular multiplication, and a separate jointly
capped box encoder improve the bound to $O(n^{2.9926863})$.
A shrinking-bias source construction and a small numerical refinement
give $O(n^{2.9926862626})$; the original geometry/query bottleneck remains.
A separate signed-window
bit-refinement reduction gives deterministic exact all-edges witness
counts, including min-plus multiplicities, in $O(n^{2.99294792})$ time
with its earlier witness. Applied to
fixed original 3SUM buckets with cyclic histogram products, it gives
deterministic 3SUM and exact \#3SUM in $O(n^{1.9931055})$ time.
Rank-prefix hashing with complementary certification gives real 3SUM and
APSP expected times $O(n^{1.996001})$ and $O(n^{2.996001})$ with the
ten-term data structure.
Rectangular products and isolated witness recovery sharpen convolution
and weighted-clique bounds; asymmetric covers also give min-sum subset
convolution in expected $O^*(2.997190^n)$ time. With square five-term
tiles, integer min-plus convolution takes $O(n^{1.993927})$ time, both
Las Vegas and deterministically, and min-sum subset convolution expected
$O^*(2.995627^n)$ time. Read on lopsided instances, the deterministic
product bound makes the source's reduction from directed APSP with
polylogarithmic weights explicit: $O(n^{2.525754})$ against Zwick's
$O(n^{2.5275})$, and $O(n^{2.5251975})$ with one further rectangular table
row. Over $\mathbb F_2$ the fixed-bucket construction gives deterministic
3XOR and exact \#3XOR in $O(n^{1.9931055})$.
We also derive deterministic and real-input corollaries, a wider thin-matrix
regime, and the displayed bounds that the source's further reductions inherit. Exact rational interval certificates check every numerical inequality
used in the stated bounds. These are mathematical derivations for independent
review, not a claim of literature priority or proof-assistant certification.
\end{abstract}

\section{Results and scope}
The source is \paper, by Josh Alman and Virginia Vassilevska Williams.
We use its algebraic identity and batch embedding (Lemmas 6--9), its tiling,
and its box construction (Lemmas 27--29). The changes proved here are the
encoding restriction, sharper counting, modular filtering and residue
folding, conditional modular moments, certified rank-prefix counts,
isolated witness recovery, and rectangular product reductions.
Except in the separately stated subset-convolution result, input integers
and weights have polynomial magnitude, with the exponent treated as a fixed
constant. The subset-convolution result explicitly charges arbitrary input
bit length. The word RAM has $O(\log N)$ or $O(\log n)$-bit
words. APSP assumes no negative cycles.

The integer randomized result uses a different guarantee from the paper's
deterministic integer headline. The real-input bounds allow comparisons,
additions, and subtractions on real numbers, as in the source reductions.
These asymptotic bounds do not assert practical speedups.
Section~\ref{sec:further} lists the bounds inherited by weighted $k$-Clique,
by the problems solved by one min-plus product or one APSP, by hinted OMv,
by min-plus convolution and MonoConvolution, and by the counting versions.

\section{Pruning the input encoding}
Write $D=4^m$, $L=\lceil cm\rceil$, $K=\binom Lm$,
$N_0=3^{L-m}$, and $M=KN_0^2=\binom Lm9^{L-m}$.
The recursion has ten child types: nine $P_{ij}$ and one $P_0$.
Each left or right input array at depth $k$ has $7^{L-k}$ entries.
An output whose inner set has size $m$ uses only leaves containing at most
$m$ occurrences of $P_0$.

\begin{lemma}[Restricted encoding]
All encoded scalars at leaves with at most $m$ occurrences of $P_0$ can be
computed in $O(S)$ arithmetic operations, where
\[
S=S(L,m)=\sum_{j=0}^m\binom Lj9^{L-j}.
\]
They can be stored in a trie of $O(S)$ vertices, with $O(L)$ lookup time.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Run only the input-encoding recursion. Do not create a $P_0$ child if its
prefix already contains $m$ copies of $P_0$. Each retained child array is the
same linear combination of its parent's seven slices as in the original
algorithm; no sibling's value is needed. Thus all retained leaf scalars are
unchanged.

Let $p_k$ be the number of retained prefixes at depth $k$. Every retained
prefix has at least $9^{L-k}$ retained leaf descendants, by choosing only
$P_{ij}$ thereafter. These descendant sets are disjoint. Therefore
\[
p_k9^{L-k}\le S,\qquad
\sum_{k=0}^Lp_k7^{L-k}
\le S\sum_{h=0}^L(7/9)^h\le \frac92 S.
\]
Forming the at most ten child arrays at a node costs $O(7^{L-k})$, proving
the time bound. Similarly $\sum_k p_k\le S\sum_{h\ge0}9^{-h}=9S/8$ bounds
the trie size. We do not allocate the old $10^L$-entry array.
Dense root initialization and the paper's $O(L7^L)$ input formation are
also $O(S)$ because $L7^L=O(9^L)$ and $9^L\le S$.
\end{proof}

Every low-order query leaf is retained. A stored box has at most $m-t$
positions marked $P_0$ or free; hence every leaf needed by its dynamic
program is retained too. All scalar values are unchanged.

For fixed $c>10$, put $\beta_d=\binom L{m-d}9^{L-m+d}$.
Then $S=\sum_{d=0}^m\beta_d$, $\beta_0=M$, and
\[
\frac{\beta_d}{\beta_{d-1}}
=\frac{9(m-d+1)}{L-m+d}
\le\frac{9m}{L-m+1}<\frac9{c-1}<1.
\]
Thus $S=O(M)$. There are $O(N/\sqrt M)$ row and column bands, so all
encodings now cost $\softO(N\sqrt M)$ instead of
$\softO(N10^L/\sqrt M)$.

\section{Exact box-tail exponents}
Let $\HH(x)=-x\ln x-(1-x)\ln(1-x)$, with natural logarithms, and define
\begin{align}
T(c)&=\frac{\tfrac12c\HH(1/c)+(c-1)\ln3}{\ln4},\\
G(c,\theta)&=\frac{c\HH(1/c)-c\HH((1-\theta)/c)-\theta\ln9}{\ln4},\\
q(\theta)&=\frac{\HH(\theta)+\theta\ln9}{\ln4},\\
E(c)&=\begin{cases}
T(c),&c\ge10,\\
c\ln10/\ln4-T(c),&1<c<10.
\end{cases}
\end{align}

\begin{theorem}[Refined data structure]
\label{thm:refined}
Fix $c>1$ and $0<\theta<0.9$ such that
\[
\frac{9(1-\theta)}{c-1+\theta}<1,\qquad G(c,\theta)>0.
\]
For $L=\lceil cm\rceil$, $t=\lceil\theta m\rceil$, and
$N\ge\sqrt K N_0$, the data structure has preprocessing time and space
\[
\softO\bigl(ND^{E(c)}+N^2D^{-G(c,\theta)}\bigr)
\]
and individual query time $\softO(D^{q(\theta)})$.
Here the suppressed factors are powers of $\log D$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Lemma 29 of the paper bounds boxes per tile by
$(m+1)\sum_{d=t}^m\beta_d$. For $d\ge t+1$, successive ratios are
eventually bounded by a constant less than one under the displayed
condition. Consequently the tail is $O(\beta_t)$, and the exact ratio is
\[
\frac{\beta_t}{M}
=9^t\frac{\binom L{m-t}}{\binom Lm}
=D^{-G(c,\theta)}\operatorname{poly}(m).
\]
Standard two-sided entropy estimates for binomial coefficients prove the
last equality up to polynomial factors. Multiplying by $O(N^2/M)$ tiles
gives the box term. Queries use
$O(L\sum_{d=0}^t\binom md9^d)$ values. Because $\theta<0.9$, the
summands are increasing up to the cutoff, and their sum is
$D^{q(\theta)}\operatorname{poly}(m)$.

For $c>10$, the encoding lemma gives $\sqrt M=D^{T(c)}\operatorname{poly}(m)$.
For $c\le10$, the maximum term in $S$ is at $j\approx L/10\le m$, so
$S=10^L\operatorname{poly}(m)$ and the encoding exponent is the second
branch of $E$. Both branches agree at $c=10$.

The input-layer count remains
$\binom Lm3^{L-m}4^m\le7^L$. The algebraic embedding and the box identities
require $L\ge m$, not $L\ge10m$. The latter condition in the paper was used
for its global geometric bound. This proof therefore also covers the
stated $c<10$ range. For fixed $c>1$, $N\ge N_0=3^{L-m}$ implies
$L=O(\log N)$; the original intermediate-value bounds remain polynomial
in $N$. Padding $D$ to a power of four and rounding $L,t$ changes constants
or polylogarithmic factors only. Small bounded $D$ is handled directly.
\end{proof}

For $D\le N^e$ and $|W|\le N^2/D^\kappa$, a sufficient strict certificate
for $O(N^2/D^g)$ is
\[
g<G,\qquad g<\kappa-q,\qquad g<1/e-E,
\qquad 1/e>T.
\tag{5}
\]
Every strict gap absorbs the suppressed logarithms.

\begin{corollary}[Same regime; larger saving]
Take $c=21.775$, $\theta=0.11121$, and $e=1/18$. Then
\[
G=0.0719572213\ldots,\quad q=0.4280411447\ldots,
\quad E+G=17.9991675570\ldots.
\]
Preprocessing takes $O(N^2/D^{0.07195})$, and queries take
$O(D^{0.42805})$. Thus $|W|\le N^2/\sqrt D$ can be answered in
$O(N^2/D^{0.07195})$.
\end{corollary}
This increases the displayed saving exponent $0.063$ by about $14.2\%$;
it is not a claim of a $14.2\%$ wall-clock speedup.

For a different tradeoff, $c=9.33$, $\theta=0.135$, $e=0.129$ satisfy (5)
with $\kappa=1/2$ and $g=0.0001$. This extends the supported inner dimension
to $D\le N^{0.129}$ at that density. It does \emph{not} extend the paper's
universal statement for every positive $\kappa$ or every positive query
exponent. The smaller saving is part of this tradeoff.

\subsection*{Batching independent products into the tensor slots}
The tiling above uses all $K=\binom Lm$ independent product slots of the
source's Lemma 9 for a grid of blocks from one matrix product. When many
independent products of the same shape are available, assigning one product
to each slot gives a different encoding amortization and a weaker outer
dimension requirement.

\begin{lemma}[Direct-sum tiling]
\label{lem:direct-sum}
Let $D=4^m$, $L=\lceil cm\rceil$ for fixed $c>10$, $K=\binom Lm$,
$N_0=3^{L-m}$, and $M=KN_0^2$. Consider $B$ independent products of
$N\times D$ by $D\times N$ integer matrices with $B\ge K$ and $N\ge N_0$, all
dimensions and magnitudes polynomial in the ambient size. The $B$ products
can be preprocessed, and their $Q_{\rm tot}$ requested entries answered, in
\[
\softO\left(BNN_0+BN^2D^{-G}+Q_{\rm tot}D^{q}\right)
\]
time, with the box exponent $G$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:refined} and either the
query exponent $q$ of that theorem or the exponent $q_{\rm pc}$ of
Lemma~\ref{lem:prefix} below.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Partition the products into packs of $K$, padding the last pack with zero
products, and fix for each pack a bijection between its slots and the
subsets $Q\subseteq[L]$ of size $m$. Partition every input's rows and
columns into common consecutive blocks of $N_0$ positions, padding the last
blocks with zeros. For each row-block index $i$ build one left array $a_i$:
in the coordinates of inner set $Q$ place row block $i$ of the left matrix
of the product assigned to $Q$; for each column-block index $j$ build one
right array $b_j$ analogously. The left array depends on $i$ and the pack
only, and the right array on $j$ and the pack only, so each retained
encoding is computed once and shared by every tile $(i,j)$.

The source's Lemma 9 permits completely independent matrices in the $K$
slots: on the tile $(i,j)$ the output with inner set $Q$ is exactly the
$(i,j)$ block of the product assigned to $Q$, because an output with inner
set $Q$ forces both input inner sets to contain $Q$ while every active input
has exactly $m$ inner positions. The assignment stays fixed across tiles,
each requested entry has a unique pack, slot, tile, and output string, and
its query runs unchanged with no sum over slots.

A pack has $\lceil N/N_0\rceil$ row and column blocks and therefore
$2\lceil N/N_0\rceil$ encodings of cost $O(S)=O(M)$ each by the restricted
encoding lemma, including input placement since $KN_0D\le7^L\le S$. Over
$\lceil B/K\rceil$ packs this is $\softO(BNN_0)$ because $B\ge K$ and
$N\ge N_0$ absorb the ceilings. Each pack has $\lceil N/N_0\rceil^2$ tiles
with the unchanged box preprocessing $\softO(MD^{-G})$, which totals
$\softO(BN^2D^{-G})$. Finally $N\ge N_0$ and fixed $c$ give $L=O(\log N)$,
so the intermediate-value bounds are unchanged.
\end{proof}
The condition $N\ge N_0$ replaces $N\ge\sqrt KN_0$ only for this layout, and
its price is the capacity condition $B\ge K$. Write
\[
A_0(c)=\frac{(c-1)\ln3}{\ln4},\qquad C_K(c)=\frac{c\HH(1/c)}{\ln4},
\]
so that $N_0=\widetilde\Theta(D^{A_0})$, $K=\widetilde\Theta(D^{C_K})$, and
$T=A_0+C_K/2$. The encoding exponent of a batched product is therefore
$A_0$ in place of $E=T$. For lopsided instances with middle part $H$, the
reductions below supply only $\widetilde\Theta(H/\sqrt D)$ independent
products per prime, so $B\ge K$ must be checked separately; the earlier
condition $H\ge\sqrt D$ alone does not permit substituting $A_0$ for $E$.

\subsection*{Contracting a prefix of each query}
The query of Theorem~\ref{thm:refined} reads $\alpha_t$ stored boxes and the
low-order leaves one by one. Boxes with few leading stars can instead be
aggregated on the fly, using only the stored encodings.

\begin{lemma}[Prefix contraction]
\label{lem:prefix}
Write $P=\varphi_{P_0}\psi_{P_0}$ and $I=\sum_{i,j\le2}p_{ij}q_{ij}$ for the
one-level bilinear kernels. The identity of the source gives
\[
\sum_{i,j\le3}\varphi_{P_{ij}}\psi_{P_{ij}}=I-P,\qquad
\sum_{\lambda}\varphi_\lambda\psi_\lambda=I,
\]
the second sum over all ten terms. Moreover $p_{ij}=\varphi_{P_{ij}}-\varphi_{P_{i3}}$
and $q_{ij}=\psi_{P_{ij}}-\psi_{P_{3j}}$ for $i,j\le2$. Fix a query with
inner set $Q$, the switching order $t$, and an integer $0\le\ell\le m-t$;
let $F$ be the first $\ell$ positions of $Q$ in the order used to define
boxes and $Q'=Q\setminus F$. Then the output equals the sum of the
$9^t\binom{m-\ell}t$ stored boxes with at least $\ell$ stars plus a
contraction computed from the retained encodings in
$\operatorname{poly}(L)\,6^\ell\sum_{j<t}\binom{m-\ell}j6^j$ operations.
For $t=\lceil\theta m\rceil$, $\ell=\lfloor rm\rfloor$, $0\le r<1-\theta$,
and $\theta/(1-r)<6/7$, the query time is $\softO(D^{q_{\rm pc}})$ with
\[
q_{\rm pc}=\max(q_1,q_2),\quad
q_1=\frac{(1-r)\HH(\theta/(1-r))+(\theta+r)\ln6}{\ln4},\quad
q_2=\frac{(1-r)\HH(\theta/(1-r))+\theta\ln9}{\ln4},
\]
For $0<r\le\theta\ln(9/6)/\ln6$, this is strictly below the
exponent $q$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:refined}. For other admissible $r$,
use the displayed maximum without asserting an improvement.
No preprocessing cost or tile condition changes.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The boxes of the query with at least $\ell$ stars cover exactly the leaves
whose restriction to $Q'$ has at least $t$ terms other than $P_0$: the
stopping rule sees its $t$-th such term before reaching $F$ exactly in this
case. For fixed $e\ge\ell$ there are $\binom{m-e-1}{t-1}$ sets $V$, hence
$9^t\sum_{e\ge\ell}\binom{m-e-1}{t-1}=9^t\binom{m-\ell}t$ such boxes; read
them from the trie. The remaining leaves have fewer than $t$ terms other
than $P_0$ on $Q'$ and arbitrary terms on $F$. With the fixed terms outside
$Q$ suppressed, their sum is the bilinear kernel
\[
I^{F}\sum_{d<t}\sum_{\substack{U\subseteq Q'\\|U|=d}}(I-P)^UP^{Q'\setminus U}
=I^{F}\sum_{\substack{A\subseteq Q'\\|A|<t}}
(-1)^{t-|A|-1}\binom{m-\ell-|A|-1}{t-|A|-1}I^AP^{Q'\setminus A},
\]
by $\sum_{d=j}^{t-1}(-1)^{d-j}\binom{m'-j}{d-j}=(-1)^{t-j-1}\binom{m'-j-1}{t-j-1}$
with $m'=m-\ell$. A contraction with kernel $I$ at the $j=\ell+|A|$
positions of $F\cup A$, $P$ at $Q'\setminus A$, and the query's terms
elsewhere is computed from $6^j$ retained encodings on each side: at each
$I$ position the six forms $\varphi_{P_{ij}}$, $i\le2$, $j\le3$, give the
four forms $p_{ij}$ by a $4\times6$ transform, and symmetrically on the
right; applying these transforms one position at a time, each shrinking its
dimension, costs $O(j6^j)$, after which the $4^j$ matching coordinates are
multiplied and summed. Every fetched leaf has at most $m-\ell-|A|\le m$
copies of $P_0$, so it is retained. Summing over $A$ gives the stated
operation count; the binomial coefficients have $O(m)$ bits, so the
intermediate-value bounds survive. With $t=\lceil\theta m\rceil$ and
$\ell=\lfloor rm\rfloor$ the terms of the residual sum increase up to the
cutoff when $\theta/(1-r)<6/7$, and entropy estimates give the two
exponents; $(1-r)\HH(\theta/(1-r))<\HH(\theta)$ for $0<r<1-\theta$, which
gives the strict comparison with $q$ when also $r\ln6\le\theta\ln(9/6)$.
The balance $q_1=q_2$ occurs at
$r=\theta\ln(9/6)/\ln6$; explicit certificates use a nearby rational $r$
and check both exponents.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Prefix-compatible encoding budget]
\label{lem:prefix-encoding-budget}
Under Lemma~\ref{lem:prefix}, suppose also $\ell\le t$ and use the
prefix-contracted algorithm for every query. It suffices to retain the
input encodings only at leaves with at most $m-\ell$ copies of $P_0$.
For fixed $c>10$ and $\ell=\lfloor rm\rfloor$, the encoding cost per
array is $\widetilde O(MD^{-G(c,r)})$. In Lemma~\ref{lem:direct-sum},
the total encoding cost is consequently
$\widetilde O(BNN_0D^{-G(c,r)})$; its geometry and capacity hypotheses,
box costs, and prefix-query costs are unchanged.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The residual contraction indexed by $A\subseteq Q'$ accesses $P_0$
only at $Q'\setminus A$: each of the six forms used by the shrinking
transform at an $I$ position is a $P_{ij}$ form, and the fixed positions
outside $Q$ also use $P_{ij}$. Every fetched leaf therefore has
$m-\ell-|A|\le m-\ell$ copies of $P_0$. Every stored box and every
intermediate box in its dynamic program has at most $m-t$ symbols that
are $P_0$ or stars. Its leaf values thus need at most $m-t\le m-\ell$
copies of $P_0$ as well.

Run the restricted input recursion with budget $m-\ell$ in place of $m$.
The identical prefix argument bounds its entire internal work and compact
trie size by a constant times
\[
S_{m-\ell}=\sum_{j=0}^{m-\ell}\binom Lj9^{L-j}
           =\sum_{d=\ell}^m\beta_d.
\]
Dense root initialization, input placement, and subset-slot enumeration
remain $O(S_{m-\ell})$, since $S_{m-\ell}\ge9^L$ and their cost is at
most $O(L7^L)$. For $c>10$, the same geometric ratio bound gives
$S_{m-\ell}=O(\beta_\ell)$, and the entropy estimate gives
\[
\frac{\beta_\ell}{M}
=9^\ell\frac{\binom L{m-\ell}}{\binom Lm}
=D^{-G(c,r)}\operatorname{poly}(m).
\]
The layout still uses $O((B/K)(N/N_0))$ encoded arrays under the original
two packing hypotheses, giving the stated total. The reduction never
uses the old direct low-order query after making this additional pruning.
\end{proof}

\subsection*{Deterministic cyclic-order lazy encoding}
\begin{lemma}[Cyclic-order lazy encoding]
\label{lem:cyclic-lazy}
Fix $c>10$ and $0<\alpha<\theta$. Let $D=4^m$, $L=\lceil cm\rceil$,
$R_0=\lfloor(1-\alpha)m\rfloor$, and
$S_{R_0}=\sum_{j=0}^{R_0}\binom Lj9^{L-j}$. The encodings needed by
Lemma~\ref{lem:prefix} can be represented deterministically in
$\widetilde O(S_{R_0})$ preprocessing time and space per input band.
All box values are preprocessed as before. A scalar leaf with $z>R_0$
copies of $P_0$ can be retrieved in
\[
\operatorname{poly}(L)3^{\lceil L(z-R_0)/z\rceil}
\]
time; leaves with $z\le R_0$ take $\operatorname{poly}(L)$ time.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For each of the $L$ cyclic rotations of the tensor coordinates, permute
both the input array and the lookup labels by that rotation. Build the
restricted encoder with budget $R_0$, storing the whole remaining input
array at every retained prefix, not only the final scalar. If $p_d$ is the
number of retained prefixes at depth $d$, every prefix has at least
$9^{L-d}$ retained descendants, so
\[
\sum_{d=0}^Lp_d7^{L-d}\le \frac92 S_{R_0}.
\]
This pays for every stored array and all recursion work. Input permutation
and initialization take $O(L7^L)=\widetilde O(S_{R_0})$ per rotation.
Coordinate permutations do not change the multilinear evaluation when
applied to both the array and its query string.

Consider a target leaf with $z>R_0$ positions labeled $P_0$, put
$s=z-R_0$, and set $R=\lceil Ls/z\rceil$. Among the $L$ cyclic windows of
length $R$, the average number of these positions is $zR/L\ge s$,
because each position belongs to exactly $R$ windows. A window therefore
contains at least $s$ positions. Rotate it to be the final $R$ coordinates.
The preceding coordinates contain at most $z-s=R_0$ copies of $P_0$, so
the first forbidden child, if any, occurs within this final window. Its
parent array is stored and has at most $R$ coordinates remaining. Every
one-level form has at most three nonzero coefficients, so directly
contracting the remaining array along the target forms reads only the
Cartesian product of their supports, at most $3^R$ entries, and costs
$\operatorname{poly}(L)3^R$. This is a sparse contraction: no dense
$7^R$ temporary array is formed. Finding the rotation and retained parent costs
only $\operatorname{poly}(L)$. This is deterministic and works for every
logical leaf, including adaptively chosen leaves.

The stored boxes need only leaves with at most $m-t$ copies of $P_0$.
Since $\alpha<\theta$, for sufficiently large $m$ these are below $R_0$;
bounded exceptional sizes can be evaluated directly. Box preprocessing
therefore uses its original recurrence and costs. The retained arrays and
direct contractions have the same polynomial intermediate-value bounds as
the original input encoder.
\end{proof}

The geometric-tail and entropy argument yields
\[
S_{R_0}=\widetilde\Theta(MD^{-G(c,\alpha)}).
\]
Thus single-product encoding exponent $E=T$ becomes $T-G(c,\alpha)$,
and the direct-sum encoding exponent $A_0$ becomes $A_0-G(c,\alpha)$.
All tiling and batching capacity conditions remain unchanged.

For a prefix-contracted query let $\ell=\lfloor rm\rfloor$, with
$0<r<\alpha<\theta$ and $\theta/(1-r)<6/7$. In the residual term indexed
by $j=xm$, every fetched leaf has $z=m-\ell-j$ copies of $P_0$. It is
already stored for $x\ge\alpha-r$, up to rounding. The remaining terms
have total exponent bounded by the maximum of
\[
\frac{(1-r)\HH(x/(1-r))+(r+x)\ln6}{\ln4}
+\frac{c\ln3}{\ln4}\frac{\alpha-r-x}{1-r-x},
\qquad 0\le x\le\alpha-r.
\]
A convenient closed upper bound is
\[
q_{\rm lazy}=r\log_4 6
 +c\log_4 3\frac{\alpha-r}{1-r}
 +(1-r)\log_4\!\left(1+6\,3^{-c(1-\alpha)/(1-r)^2}\right).
\]
Indeed, with $h=1-r$ and $\tau=\alpha-r$, concavity gives
$(\tau-x)/(h-x)\le\tau/h-(h-\tau)x/h^2$, and maximizing the resulting
entropy-plus-linear expression over $0\le x\le h$ gives the displayed
bound. All rounding errors in the omitted-leaf range change the exponent of a
lookup by $O(1/m)$, since $1-r-x\ge1-\alpha>0$; they therefore contribute
only constant or polynomial factors in $L$. The complete query exponent is
$\max(q_1,q_2,q_{\rm lazy})$, where $q_1,q_2$ are the two original prefix
exponents. In particular, if $q_{\rm lazy}<\max(q_1,q_2)$, the old query
bound holds with the strictly smaller encoding exponent above.

\section{Independent modular filters remove the scan loss}
The following reduction changes the algorithm, independently of the
encoding modification. Write $S(a,b,c)=w(a,b)+w(a,c)+w(b,c)$.
Its two ingredients are a folding of the residue classes of a modulus into
the outer dimensions of one thin product, which shares the band encodings
of the data structure across residues, and the intersection of independent
modular filters before any exhaustive scan.

\begin{lemma}[Residue folding]
\label{lem:fold}
Fix a prime $p$ and a block $B$ of the third vertex part, and put
$p_1=\lceil\sqrt p\,\rceil$ and $p_2=\lceil p/p_1\rceil$, so that
$p\le p_1p_2$ and $\max(p_1,p_2)\le\sqrt p+1$. Index rows by the pairs
$(a,\rho_1)$ with $0\le\rho_1<p_1$, columns by the pairs $(b,\rho_2)$ with
$0\le\rho_2<p_2$, and the middle part by $B\times\mathbb Z_p$. Define the
Boolean matrices
\begin{align*}
X[(a,\rho_1),(c,\sigma)]&=1\iff\sigma\equiv w(a,c)+\rho_1\pmod p,\\
Y[(c,\sigma),(b,\rho_2)]&=1\iff\sigma\equiv-w(b,c)-p_1\rho_2\pmod p.
\end{align*}
For an outer pair $(a,b)$, write $w(a,b)\bmod p=\rho_1+p_1\rho_2$ with
$0\le\rho_1<p_1$; then $0\le\rho_2<p_2$, and
\[
(XY)[(a,\rho_1),(b,\rho_2)]=\#\{c\in B:\ S(a,b,c)\equiv0\pmod p\}.
\]
The product has outer dimensions at most $n(\sqrt p+1)$ and inner dimension
$|B|\,p$, and the $n^2$ outer pairs are queried at $n^2$ distinct
positions.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The entry counts the $c\in B$ with
$w(a,c)+\rho_1\equiv-w(b,c)-p_1\rho_2$, that is, with
$w(a,c)+w(b,c)+w(a,b)\equiv0\pmod p$, because
$\rho_1+p_1\rho_2\equiv w(a,b)$. Since $0\le w(a,b)\bmod p<p\le p_1p_2$,
the digits $\rho_1,\rho_2$ exist and are unique, and distinct pairs
$(a,b)$ occupy distinct positions. Finally $p_1\le\sqrt p+1$ and
$p_2\le p/\sqrt p+1$.
\end{proof}
The lemma replaces the $p$ residue-specific instances of a block, each an
$n\times n$ product, by one product with outer dimension
$N=O(n\sqrt p)$. The box preprocessing $N^2D^{-G}$ is unchanged in total,
and the queries are unchanged, but the band encodings cost $ND^E$ instead
of $pnD^E$: they are shared across all residues. The same effect can be
obtained with rows $(a,\rho)$ for all $\rho\in\mathbb Z_p$ and unchanged
columns, using rectangular tiles with $\sqrt{pK}\times\sqrt{K/p}$ block
products; the square version above needs no change to the data structure.

\begin{theorem}[Amplified reduction with folding]
\label{thm:amplified}
Suppose a thin-product data structure preprocesses $N\times D$ by
$D\times N$ integer matrices in $\softO(ND^E+N^2D^{-G})$ time and answers
an entry in $\softO(D^q)$ time whenever $N\ge D^T\operatorname{poly}(\log D)$,
where $G>0$, $q<1/2$, and $E,T>0$ are constants. For a fixed $0<e<1$
with $D=n^e$ and $e(T-1/4)<1$, all-edges Exact Triangle has a Las Vegas
algorithm with expected time
\[
\softO\left(n^2D^{E-1/4}+n^3D^{-G}+n^3D^{q-1/2}+n^2D^{3/4}+n^2\sqrt D\right).
\tag{6}
\]
Without Lemma~\ref{lem:fold}, the same proof with $p$ residue-specific
instances per block gives the encoding term $n^2D^E$, the construction term
$n^2D$, and the condition $eT<1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $\ell=\log n$, $P=\lceil\sqrt D\,\ell^2\rceil$,
$h=\lfloor D/(2P)\rfloor$, and $k=\lceil4\log_2 n\rceil$.
Partition the third vertex part into blocks of at most $h$ vertices.
Sieve the primes in $[P,2P]$ and sample $k$ of them uniformly and
independently with replacement. They and the blocks are fixed before queries.

For each sampled prime $p$ and block $B$, construct the folded instance of
Lemma~\ref{lem:fold}. Its middle dimension is at most $2Ph\le D$ and its
outer dimensions are at most $n(\sqrt{2P}+1)$; pad it with zeros to a square
$N\times D$ by $D\times N$ product with $N=\Theta(n\sqrt P)$. Since
$N\ge n\sqrt P\ge nD^{1/4}\log n$ and $e(T-1/4)<1$, the hypothesis
$N\ge D^T\operatorname{poly}(\log D)$ holds for all sufficiently large $n$. For every outer pair, compute
$w(a,b)\bmod p$ once per sampled prime, and query the single position
$((a,\rho_1),(b,\rho_2))$ of the lemma in every block. The residue classes
need not be balanced: the data structure charges each entry query
separately. Thus each pair is queried exactly once per prime and block.
A pair/block passes a prime if some $c$ in that block
makes $S(a,b,c)=0\pmod p$. Scan a block using the original integer sums
only if it passes \emph{all} sampled primes. Stop scanning a given pair
after its first exact zero witness is found.

Every genuine zero passes every prime, and marking a pair requires an
explicit exact check. The algorithm is therefore always correct. Passing
all filters alone is not a certificate: different primes may have different
modular witnesses in the block.

Fix a zero-free pair/block. Each nonzero sum, of magnitude at most
$3n^\nu$, has at most $\log(3n^\nu)/\log P$ prime divisors at least $P$.
There are $\Omega(P/\log P)$ primes in the interval. A union bound over
at most $h$ sums gives
\[
\Pr[\text{pass one prime}]
\le C_\nu h\log n/P=O_\nu((\log n)^{-3}).
\]
For all sufficiently large $n$, this is at most $1/2$. Independence across
prime choices gives survival probability at most $2^{-k}\le n^{-4}$.
There are $O(n^3/h)$ pair/blocks, so their total expected failed-scan cost
is $O(n^3n^{-4})$. At most $n^2$ successful scans occur, costing $O(n^2h)$.
No independence between different pair/blocks is assumed.

Let $b=\lceil n/h\rceil$ be the number of blocks. There are
$kb=\softO(n/\sqrt D)$ folded instances, each with $N=\softO(nD^{1/4})$,
since $P=\softO(\sqrt D)$. Their band encodings cost
$\softO(kbND^E)=\softO(n^2D^{E-1/4})$, and their box preprocessing costs
$\softO(kbN^2D^{-G})=\softO(n^3(P/h)D^{-G})=\softO(n^3D^{-G})$, since
$P/h=\Theta(\log^4n)$. The number of entry queries is
$kbn^2=\softO(n^3/h)=\softO(n^3D^{-1/2})$, because each pair is queried
once per prime and block. These give the first three terms of (6).
Writing the two Boolean matrices of every instance explicitly costs
$\softO(kbND)=\softO(n^2D^{3/4})$.
The sieve, the residues $w(a,b)\bmod p$, and combining the answers are
smaller. Successful scans give the last term. Processing one block at a
time suffices.
Finitely many small sizes can use brute force. Randomness affects time only.
This computes all answers for the designated outer-edge class. If answers
for all three edge classes are required, repeat with the three choices of
middle part, changing the cost by a constant factor.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Integer all-edges Exact Triangle]
Take $c=21.0477$, $\theta=0.111971$, and $e=0.05828$ in the refined
data structure of Theorem~\ref{thm:refined}, with the explicit
rounding $D=4^{\lfloor\log_4(n^e)\rfloor}=\Theta(n^e)$. Its tiling
condition $N\ge\sqrt K N_0=\sqrt M$ holds for the folded instances, whose
outer dimension is $N=\Theta(n\sqrt P)\ge nD^{1/4}$, for all sufficiently
large $n$, since
\[
e(T-1/4)=0.995905586384774\ldots<1,
\qquad \sqrt M=\widetilde\Theta(D^T).
\]
This uses the modified parameterized construction: $e>1/18$, so the
convenient $N\ge D^{18}$ corollary alone does not cover these parameters.
The strict savings are
\[
eG=0.004057096756\ldots,\qquad
e(1/2-q)=0.004057095599\ldots,\qquad
1-e(E-1/4)=0.004094413615\ldots,
\]
all above $0.004057$. All-edges Exact Triangle therefore
has expected running time $O(n^{3-0.004057})=O(n^{2.995943})$.
\end{corollary}
The numerical supremum of this parameter family, about $0.0040572$, is
\emph{not} claimed. The chosen strict gaps also absorb the extra logarithms
introduced by amplification. Without Lemma~\ref{lem:fold}, the parameters
$c=21.2733$, $\theta=0.111731$, $e=0.056846$ satisfy the unfolded
certificate with saving $0.003999$, giving $O(n^{2.996001})$; the companion
script certifies both parameter sets.

For clarity, the complete expected bound is
\[
\softO\left(n^2D^{E-1/4}+n^3D^{-G}+n^3D^{q-1/2}+n^2D^{3/4}+n^2\sqrt D\right).
\]
Its five exponents after setting $D=n^{0.05828}$ are:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule
Cost & Exponent of $n$, before logarithmic factors \\
\midrule
Encodings, $n^2D^{E-1/4}$ & $2.995905586384774\ldots$ \\
Boxes, $n^3D^{-G}$ & $2.995942903243984\ldots$ \\
Queries, $n^3D^{q-1/2}$ & $2.995942904400685\ldots$ \\
Matrix construction, $n^2D^{3/4}$ & $2.04371$ \\
Successful scans, $n^2\sqrt D$ & $2.02914$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Let $\tau$ denote the maximum of these exact exponents. Thus
\[
\tau=3-e(1/2-q)=2.995942904400685\ldots,
\quad 2.995943-\tau=0.000000095599315\ldots>0.
\]
We retain the stronger intermediate bound $\softO(n^\tau)$ through the
remaining reductions, and round the exponent only at the end.

\begin{corollary}[Batched folded reduction]
\label{cor:batched}
For each sampled prime, the $B=\lceil n/h\rceil=\widetilde\Theta(n/\sqrt D)$
folded middle-block products of Theorem~\ref{thm:amplified} are independent
and have the common shape $N\times D$ by $D\times N$ with
$N=\widetilde\Theta(nD^{1/4})$. Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:direct-sum} to them,
with the prefix-contracted queries of Lemma~\ref{lem:prefix}. The modular
answers, the filter analysis, and the scans are unchanged, and the expected
time becomes
\[
\softO\left(n^2D^{A_0-1/4}+n^3D^{-G}+n^3D^{q_{\rm pc}-1/2}+n^2D^{3/4}+n^2\sqrt D\right),
\]
valid when $nD^{1/4}\ge N_0$ and $n/\sqrt D\ge K$ up to polylogarithms, that
is, $e(A_0-1/4)<1$ and $e(C_K+1/2)<1$ for $D=n^e$. Take
\[
(c,\theta,r,e)=(20.0337,\;0.11371328,\;0.025732677,\;0.06711237).
\]
Then $A_0=15.083850\ldots$, $C_K=2.865215\ldots$,
$q_1=0.4334671995\ldots$, $q_2=0.4334671992\ldots$, and the savings are
\begin{gather*}
eG=0.004465176\ldots,\qquad e(1/2-q_1)=0.004465173\ldots,\\
e(1/2-q_2)=0.004465173\ldots,\qquad 1-e(A_0-1/4)=0.004465145\ldots,
\end{gather*}
all above $0.004465$, with $1-e(C_K+1/2)=0.774\ldots>0$. The maximal
exact cost exponent is
\[
\tau_{\rm b}=2+e(A_0-1/4)=2.995534854890842\ldots,\qquad
2.995535-\tau_{\rm b}=0.000000145109157\ldots>0,
\]
so all-edges Exact Triangle has expected running time $O(n^{2.995535})$.
\end{corollary}
The unbatched bound $O(n^{2.995943})$ remains the one to use when fewer than
$K$ independent products of a common shape are available, as in the
lopsided corollaries of Section~\ref{sec:further}. The supremum of the
batched family, about $0.0044652$, is not claimed; using the encoder budget
$m-\ell$ instead of $m$ would add about $4\cdot10^{-6}$ and is also not
claimed.

\subsection*{Keeping the full saving for integer APSP}
Here is a direct proof of the tight transfer noted in the source's footnote 10.
For each individual min-plus product, choose constants $u,v$ making its
finite integer matrices $\bar A=A+u$ and $\bar B=B+v$ entrywise nonnegative.
Since each candidate sum uses one entry from each matrix,
\[
\bar A\star\bar B=(A\star B)+(u+v).
\]
The shifts are local to this matrix product, and are removed from its
answer. Relabel the nonnegative matrices $A,B$ and recursively compute
\[
C'=\lfloor A/2\rfloor\star\lfloor B/2\rfloor,
\]
where $\star$ denotes min-plus multiplication. For every candidate index
$k$, $A_{ik}+B_{kj}\ge2C'_{ij}$, and an index attaining $C'_{ij}$ gives
$A_{ik}+B_{kj}\le2C'_{ij}+2$. Thus each integer entry of $C=A\star B$ lies
in $\{2C'_{ij},2C'_{ij}+1,2C'_{ij}+2\}$.

For $r=0,1$, an all-edges Exact Triangle call with edge weights $A_{ik}$,
$B_{kj}$, and $-(2C'_{ij}+r)$ returns a Boolean answer $Z^{(r)}_{ij}$.
Reconstruct the minimum with the explicit priority rule
\[
C_{ij}=\begin{cases}
2C'_{ij},&Z^{(0)}_{ij}=1,\\
2C'_{ij}+1,&Z^{(0)}_{ij}=0\text{ and }Z^{(1)}_{ij}=1,\\
2C'_{ij}+2,&\text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\]
Both tests can succeed through different candidates, so the first has
priority. There is one recursive min-plus call per bit level, followed by
two all-edges calls. The recursion ends at the all-zero matrices after
$O(\log U)$ levels for $U=n^{O(1)}$.
Thus only logarithmically many all-edges calls are needed, with polynomial
integer weights. Fresh randomness in each call preserves the expected-time
bound even though the calls are adaptive.

Repeated min-plus squaring with a zero diagonal computes APSP in at most
$\lceil\log_2 n\rceil$ products. If finite input weights satisfy $|w|\le W$, use an
integer sentinel $B>4nW+1$ for absent edges. Every walk considered has at
most $2n$ edges: a walk with no sentinel has magnitude at most $2nW$, and
a walk containing a sentinel has value above $2nW$. Classify final values
above $2nW$ as infinity. With no negative cycles, shortest paths can be
chosen simple. Bit scaling and repeated squaring add only logarithmic
factors to the intermediate bounds $\softO(n^\tau)$ and
$\softO(n^{\tau_{\rm b}})$. For each fixed $r$, the strict gaps
$\tau<2.995943$ and $\tau_{\rm b}<2.995535$ give
\[
n^\tau(\log n)^r=O(n^{2.995943}),\qquad
n^{\tau_{\rm b}}(\log n)^r=O(n^{2.995535}).
\]
Consequently integer min-plus product and APSP inherit expected
$O(n^{2.995535})$ time from the batched reduction, and $O(n^{2.995943})$
from the unbatched one, with the exponents rounded after both reductions.

\subsection*{Singleton decoding removes the successful-scan term}
The remaining scan term can be removed in expectation without changing the
thin-product data structure. The additional ingredient is randomized
isolation of one genuine witness, followed by an exact verification and a
fallback scan.

\begin{lemma}[Isolated witness recovery]
\label{lem:isolation}
Let the outer parts have at most $R$ vertices each and the middle part have
$H$ vertices, all polynomial in an ambient size $n$, with polynomially
bounded integer weights. Suppose the thin-product data structure has
preprocessing cost $\softO(ND^E+N^2D^{-G})$, query cost $\softO(D^q)$,
and the tile requirement $N\ge D^T\operatorname{poly}(\log D)$,
where $G>0$, $0\le q<1/2$, and $E,T>0$ are fixed. Let
$D=\Theta(n^d)$ for any fixed $d>0$, rounded to a power of four, and
suppose $H\ge\sqrt D$ and $RD^{1/4}\ge D^{T+\eta}$ for a fixed
$\eta>0$. Then all-edges Exact Triangle has an always-correct algorithm
with expected running time
\begin{equation}
\softO\left(RHD^{E-1/4}+R^2HD^{-G}
             +R^2HD^{q-1/2}+RHD^{3/4}\right).
\label{eq:isolation-costs}
\end{equation}
The same bound, with additional logarithmic factors, applies to the
$R\times H$ by $H\times R$ integer min-plus product.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Use the original parameters $P=\lceil\sqrt D\log^2 n\rceil$ and
$h=\lfloor D/(2P)\rfloor$. Choose a sufficiently large fixed $L$ and
retain $\lceil L\log_2n\rceil$ independent original modular filters
for every block of at most $h$ middle vertices. In addition, for
every block and each $j=0,\ldots,\lceil\log_2h\rceil$, perform
$C\log n$ independent trials. A trial retains each middle vertex with
probability $2^{-j}$ and chooses a fresh independent uniform prime from
$[P,2P]$. Use the usual folded instance, setting the entries of one input
associated with unretained vertices to zero. Query both its integer
product and a second product obtained by multiplying the other input's
entries associated with vertex $c$ by its distinct label $\lambda(c)$ in
$\{1,\ldots,H\}$. These return the retained modular-witness count and the
sum of the corresponding labels. If the count is one, the label sum
identifies the sole modular witness. Check its original integer sum and
mark the pair only when that sum is zero.

Fix a pair/block containing $z\ge1$ genuine witnesses, and put
$j=\lceil\log_2 z\rceil$ and $t=2^{-j}$. The probability of retaining
exactly one genuine witness is $zt(1-t)^{z-1}\ge1/(2\exp(1))$; for $z=1$
it is one. Conditional on any fixed mask with one genuine witness, at most
$h-1$ nonzero sums remain. The prime-divisor bound in
Theorem~\ref{thm:amplified} shows that a fresh independent prime avoids
all of them with probability at least $1/2$ for sufficiently large $n$.
Thus each trial at the indicated level decodes a genuine witness with
probability at least $1/(4\exp(1))$. Choosing the constant $C$ sufficiently
large makes failure in every trial at most $n^{-L}$ for any fixed desired
$L$. No independence across distinct pairs or blocks is required.

After the trials, exhaustively scan every still-unsolved pair/block that
passed all the original modular filters. A zero-free block reaches this
fallback with probability at most $n^{-L}$ by the original amplification.
A block with a genuine witness reaches it only if all its isolation trials
failed, also with probability at most $n^{-L}$. Summing the actual block
sizes bounds the expected total fallback cost by $R^2Hn^{-L}=o(1)$ for
sufficiently large fixed $L$. A genuine witness always passes the original
filters, so it is either decoded and exactly checked or found in a fallback
scan. This proves correctness for every random outcome, including the
outcome in which all isolation trials fail.

There are only polylogarithmically many products per block. The two products
per trial have outer dimension $\softO(RD^{1/4})$, inner dimension at most
$D$, and one query per outer pair. Their weighted entries have polynomial
magnitude, so the existing integer word-size bounds apply. With
$H\ge\sqrt D\ge h$, there are $O(H/h)=\softO(HD^{-1/2})$ blocks, giving
exactly the four terms in \eqref{eq:isolation-costs}. Mask generation, label decoding, and exact
checks fit these costs. For $H<h$, one must instead retain
$\lceil H/h\rceil$ in the number of instances; the hypothesis is not
removed by isolation. The bit-scaling transfer applies without any change
to the two outer dimensions or the middle dimension. Fresh randomness in
each call preserves the expected bound under its adaptive calls.
\end{proof}


\subsection*{Integer 3SUM without the Exact Triangle detour}
The source's Theorem 21(a) reduces 3SUM to Exact Triangle on $n^{1/2}$
vertices and halves the saving. Its footnote 10 notes that the direct
randomized reduction of Kopelowitz, Pettie, and Porat~\cite{KPP16} to offline
set disjointness keeps all of it. That reduction splits a hash value into a
high and a low digit placed on the two sides of a set-disjointness query,
which is Lemma~\ref{lem:fold} applied to a middle part $\mathbb Z_p$. We
state it with residues modulo primes, with the balance of the buckets handled
by sub-buckets, so that it uses only the tools already in this note.
Throughout, 3SUM is given sets $A,B,C$ of at most $n$ integers of absolute
value at most $n^\nu$ and asks for $x\in A$, $y\in B$, $z\in C$ with $x+y=z$.
The one-set form, which asks for three distinct elements of a set $S$ with
$x+y+z=0$, is the instance $A=B=S$, $C=-S$ in which witnesses with a
repeated element are skipped during scans. A witness with a repeated
element is determined by one of its elements, so there are at most $3n$ of
them; a sub-query whose only witnesses are of this kind is scanned without
success, and at most $3n$ such scans occur in either algorithm below.

\begin{lemma}[Bucket-pair folding]
\label{lem:bucket}
Let $r$ and $p$ be distinct primes, $p_1=\lceil\sqrt p\,\rceil$,
$p_2=\lceil p/p_1\rceil$, and $s\ge1$. Partition $A$ into the residue
buckets $A_i=\{x\in A:x\equiv i\pmod r\}$ and cut every bucket into
sub-buckets of at most $s$ elements; do the same for $B$. Index rows by the
pairs $(\alpha,\rho_1)$ with $\alpha$ a sub-bucket of $A$ and
$0\le\rho_1<p_1$, columns by the pairs $(\beta,\rho_2)$ with $\beta$ a
sub-bucket of $B$ and $0\le\rho_2<p_2$, and the middle part by $\mathbb Z_p$.
Define the Boolean matrices
\begin{align*}
X[(\alpha,\rho_1),\sigma]&=1\iff\sigma\equiv x-\rho_1\pmod p
\text{ for some }x\in\alpha,\\
Y[\sigma,(\beta,\rho_2)]&=1\iff\sigma\equiv p_1\rho_2-y\pmod p
\text{ for some }y\in\beta.
\end{align*}
For $z\in C$ write $z\bmod p=\rho_1+p_1\rho_2$ with $0\le\rho_1<p_1$. Then
$(XY)[(\alpha,\rho_1),(\beta,\rho_2)]>0$ if and only if some $x\in\alpha$ and
$y\in\beta$ satisfy $x+y\equiv z\pmod p$. If $x+y=z$ with $x\in A_i$, then
$y\in B_{(z-i)\bmod r}$. The product has at most $(n/s+r)(\sqrt p+1)$ rows,
as many columns, and inner dimension $p$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A common middle vertex $\sigma$ exists if and only if
$x-\rho_1\equiv p_1\rho_2-y$, that is, $x+y\equiv\rho_1+p_1\rho_2\equiv z
\pmod p$. Reduction modulo $r$ is additive, which gives the bucket statement.
A bucket of $|A_i|$ elements has $\lceil|A_i|/s\rceil\le|A_i|/s+1$
sub-buckets, so there are at most $n/s+r$ of them, and
$p_1,p_2\le\sqrt p+1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Las Vegas integer 3SUM]
\label{thm:3sum}
Let the data structure satisfy the hypothesis of
Theorem~\ref{thm:amplified}, and fix $0<e<1$ with $D=n^e$ and $eT<1$.
Integer 3SUM has a Las Vegas algorithm with expected time
\[
\softO\left(nD^E+n^2D^{-G}+n^2D^{q-1/2}+n^2D^{-1/2}+nD\right).
\tag{8}
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $P=\lfloor D/2\rfloor$, $s=\lfloor\sqrt P/(C_\nu\log n)\rfloor$ for a
constant $C_\nu$ fixed below, and $R=\lceil n/s\rceil$.

\emph{Bucket prime.} For every prime $r\in[R,2R]$ compute
$\Phi(r)=\sum_i(|A_i|^2+|B_i|^2)$ in $O(n)$ time and keep a minimizer. A
nonzero difference of two inputs has at most $\log(2n^\nu)/\log R$ prime
divisors in $[R,2R]$, which contains $\Omega(R/\log R)$ primes, so the
average of $\Phi$ over these primes is $2n+O(n^2\log n/R)=O(ns\log n)$, and so
is its minimum. This costs $O(nR)=\softO(n^2D^{-1/2})$.

\emph{Universe primes.} Sieve $[P,2P]$, exclude $r$ from the candidates,
and sample
$k=\lceil4\log_2n\rceil$ of the remaining primes uniformly and
independently with replacement. For each sampled prime $p$ build the instance of
Lemma~\ref{lem:bucket} and pad it to a square $N\times D$ by $D\times N$
product with $N=O((n/s)\sqrt P)=O(n\log n)$. Since $N\ge n$ and $eT<1$, the
tile condition holds for large $n$. For every $z\in C$, residue $i$,
sub-bucket $\alpha\subseteq A_i$, and sub-bucket
$\beta\subseteq B_{(z-i)\bmod r}$, query the position of the lemma in every
instance. Scan the sub-query $(z,\alpha,\beta)$, by testing $z-x\in\beta$ for
each $x\in\alpha$ in a sorted copy of $\beta$, only if its entry is positive
in all $k$ instances, and stop at the first exact solution.

\emph{Correctness.} A solution $x+y=z$ with $x\in A_i$ has $y\in B_{z-i}$ by
the lemma and satisfies the congruence modulo every prime, so its sub-query
is positive in every instance and is scanned; a scan reports only exact
solutions. The output is therefore always correct.

\emph{Queries.} Using $|A_i||B_j|\le(|A_i|^2+|B_j|^2)/2$, the number of
sub-queries is
\[
\sum_{z\in C}\sum_i\Big\lceil\frac{|A_i|}s\Big\rceil
\Big\lceil\frac{|B_{z-i}|}s\Big\rceil
\le\frac{n\Phi(r)}{2s^2}+\frac{2n^2}s+nr=O\Big(\frac{n^2\log n}s\Big)
=\softO(n^2D^{-1/2}),
\]
each costing $\softO(D^q)$ per instance.

\emph{Failed scans.} A sub-query without an exact solution has at most $s^2$
nonzero values $x+y-z$, of magnitude at most $3n^\nu$, each with at most
$\log(3n^\nu)/\log P$ prime divisors in $[P,2P]$. By the union bound it is
positive for a uniformly sampled prime with probability at most
$s^2C'_\nu\log n/P\le1/2$ for $C_\nu$ large enough, and independence over
the $k$ primes gives survival probability at most $n^{-4}$. With
$\softO(n^2/s)$ sub-queries, the expected cost of failed scans is
$\softO((n^2/s)\,n^{-4}\,s)=o(1)$.

\emph{Costs.} Per instance, with $N=O(n\log n)$: band encodings
$\softO(nD^E)$, boxes $\softO(n^2D^{-G})$, and writing the two Boolean
matrices $\softO(nD)$. The queries cost $\softO(n^2D^{q-1/2})$, the bucket
prime $\softO(n^2D^{-1/2})$, the sieve $\softO(D)$, sorting $\softO(n)$, and
the single successful scan $\softO(s)$. These are the terms of (8).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Integer 3SUM]
Take $c=21.2733$, $\theta=0.111731$, and $e=0.056846$ in
Theorem~\ref{thm:refined}. Then
\[
eG=0.003999294\ldots,\quad e(1/2-q)=0.003999284\ldots,\quad
1-eE=0.004000455\ldots,
\]
all above $0.003999$; the terms $nD$ and $n^2D^{-1/2}$ have savings
$1-e$ and $e/2$, and $1-eT>0$. Integer 3SUM therefore has a Las Vegas
algorithm with expected time $O(n^{2-0.003999})=O(n^{1.996001})$.
\end{corollary}
These are the unfolded parameters of Theorem~\ref{thm:amplified}, and the
encoding relief $D^{-1/4}$ is not available here: a sub-query compares $s^2$
sums, so false-positive control forces $s^2\le p/\operatorname{polylog}$,
the whole inner dimension is spent on residues, and the outer dimension is
pinned to $\widetilde\Theta(n)$. The supremum of this constraint set, about
$0.0039993$, is not claimed. For integer inputs this improves the
earlier real-input bound $O(n^{1.99632})$. The rank-prefix construction below
brings real 3SUM to the same $O(n^{1.996001})$ expected bound.

\section{Deterministic and real-input corollaries}
The deterministic constructions and the real-input constructions use
different guarantees. Their full reduction algebra is included below.

\subsection*{Deterministic integer reduction}
Choose modulus scale $p=\Theta(n^a)$ and third-part block size
$h=\Theta(n^b)$ independently. Put $e=a+b$, $z=a-b>0$.
As in Theorem 17 of the source, choose a prime minimizing modular triangle
count, which also minimizes the false-positive count. It satisfies
$F(p)=O(n^3\log n/p)$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:fold} there are $O(n/h)$ folded
instances, one per block, of outer dimension $O(n\sqrt p)$ and actual inner
dimension $O(ph)$, and $O(n^3/h)$ queries. No equal-sized chunking of the
residue classes is needed because queries are charged individually. The
relevant savings below exponent $3$ are
\[
eG-z,\qquad e(1/2-q)-z/2,\qquad z,
\tag{7}
\]
from boxes, queries, and failed scans. The shared encoding term
$\softO((n/h)\,n\sqrt p\,D^E)$ additionally has saving
$1-e(E-1/4)-3z/4$, and the folded tiles fit when $n\sqrt p\ge\sqrt M$.
Prime selection with Strassen costs $O(n^{\log_2 7+3a})$; explicit
construction costs $O(n^{2+3a/2})$.
At most one successful scan per pair adds $O(n^{2+b})$.
All these terms must be checked, not only (7).

Take
\[
(c,\theta,e,z)=(21.6844,\;0.1163026,\;0.0565835,\;0.0021252).
\]
The three quantities in (7) are respectively
$0.002125821042\ldots$, $0.002125622860\ldots$, and $0.0021252$, and the
encoding saving is $0.002317406777\ldots$.
The remaining costs have strict slack as certified by the companion script,
including slack for the logarithmically many min-plus and APSP calls.
Thus deterministic all-edges Exact Triangle and the preceding min-plus/APSP
transfer take $O(n^{2.997875})$ time with one chosen prime; the per-pair
conditional moments of Corollary~\ref{cor:det-all-edges-moments} improve
this to $O(n^{2.996261})$. The source's Theorem 21(a) gives
3SUM time $n^{2-0.002125/2+o(1)}$, hence the safely rounded
$O(n^{1.998938})$. Without folding, the parameters
$(21.915,\,0.116108,\,0.0551975,\,0.0020952)$ give $O(n^{2.997905})$ and
$O(n^{1.998953})$, as also certified.

\subsection*{Deterministic integer 3SUM}
The bucket-pair reduction also derandomizes, with one modulus chosen as in
the source's Theorem 17 and the failed scans charged to false positives.
\begin{theorem}[Deterministic integer 3SUM]
\label{thm:det3sum}
With the data structure of Theorem~\ref{thm:refined}, choose
$D=\Theta(n^e)$, a sub-bucket size $s=\Theta(n^b)$ with $z:=e-2b>0$, and
$R=\lceil n/s\rceil$. Integer 3SUM has a deterministic algorithm with
running time
\[
\softO\left(n^{1+z/2}D^E+n^{2+z}D^{-G}+n^{2+z/2}D^{q-1/2}+n^{2-z}
+n^{1+3e/2+z/2}+n^{2-b}+n^{1+z/2}D\right),
\]
provided $n^{1+z/2}\ge D^T\operatorname{poly}(\log D)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Choose the bucket prime $r\in[R,2R]$ as in Theorem~\ref{thm:3sum}, in
$O(nR)=O(n^{2-b})$ time, so that $\Phi(r)=O(ns\log n)$. Let $P=\Theta(D)$
with $2P\le D$. For every prime $p\in[P,2P]$ compute
\[
C(p)=\#\{(x,y,z)\in A\times B\times C:\ x+y\equiv z\pmod{rp}\}
\]
by one exact cyclic convolution of length $rp$ of the residue histograms of
$A$ and $B$ modulo $rp$, followed by a sum over $z\in C$; with Kronecker
substitution and Sch\"onhage--Strassen multiplication this takes
$\softO(rp)$ per prime and $\softO(Prp)=\softO(n^{1-b+2e})=\softO(n^{1+3e/2+z/2})$
in all. Keep a minimizer $p$ among the candidates other than $r$.
Removing at most one prime preserves the asymptotic averaging bound. The exact
solutions satisfy every congruence, so $C(p)=F(p)+Z_0$ with $Z_0$
independent of $p$ and $F(p)$ the number of false triples, which the
minimizer therefore minimizes. A false triple has $x+y\equiv z\pmod r$,
hence is one of the $T=\sum_z\sum_i|A_i||B_{z-i}|\le n\Phi(r)/2=O(n^2s\log n)$
bucket-compatible triples, and its nonzero value $x+y-z$ has at most
$\log(3n^\nu)/\log P$ prime divisors in $[P,2P]$, which contains
$\Omega(P/\log P)$ primes. Averaging gives
$F(p)=O(T\log n/P)=O(n^2s\log^2n/P)$.

Build the instance of Lemma~\ref{lem:bucket} for $(r,p,s)$, whose outer
dimension is $N=O((n/s)\sqrt p)=O(n^{1+z/2})$, query the
$\softO(n^2/s)=\softO(n^{2-b})$ compatible sub-queries once, scan a sub-query
if its entry is positive, and stop at the first exact solution. Every
solution lies in a compatible sub-query with a positive entry, and scans are
exact, so the algorithm is correct. A failed scan $(z,\alpha,\beta)$ contains
a false triple $(x,y,z)$ with $x\in\alpha$ and $y\in\beta$, and distinct
failed scans contain distinct triples because the sub-buckets partition $A$
and $B$; so failed scans cost $O(F(p)\,s\log n)=\softO(n^2s^2/P)=\softO(n^{2-z})$.
The remaining terms are the band encodings $ND^E$, the boxes $N^2D^{-G}$,
the queries $n^{2-b}D^q=n^{2+z/2}D^{q-1/2}$, writing the matrices $ND$, and
one successful scan.
\end{proof}

Writing $\delta$ for the saving below exponent $2$, the certificate reads
\begin{gather*}
eG-z>\delta,\qquad e(1/2-q)-z/2>\delta,\qquad z>\delta,\qquad
1-eE-z/2>\delta,\\
1-3e/2-z/2>\delta,\qquad (e-z)/2>\delta,\qquad 1-e-z/2>\delta,\qquad
1+z/2-eT>0.
\end{gather*}
The first three are the Exact Triangle quantities (7); the encoding
constraint lies between the unfolded and the folded Exact Triangle versions.
\begin{corollary}[Deterministic integer 3SUM]
Take
\[
(c,\theta,e,z)=(21.915,\;0.116108,\;0.055255,\;0.0020975).
\]
The savings are $0.002097422\ldots$, $0.002097397\ldots$, $0.0020975$, and
$0.002122108\ldots$ for boxes, queries, failed scans, and encodings, with
the other terms far larger; all exceed $0.002097$. Integer 3SUM therefore
has a deterministic algorithm with running time $O(n^{1.997903})$. The
source's Theorem 21(a) with the deterministic Exact Triangle bound gives
only $O(n^{1.998938})$.
\end{corollary}
The supremum of this constraint set, about $0.0020974$, is not claimed.
This single-modulus construction must absorb all failed scans. The next
construction instead chooses several primes by an exact conditional
objective, and may certify YES before any scanning.

\subsection*{Deterministic integer 3SUM by conditional modular moments}
The preceding single-modulus choice can be strengthened deterministically.
The new ingredient is an exact objective for the intersection of several
modular filters, allowing a different witness at each prime. Its minimization
either gives a bounded scan budget or certifies an affirmative answer.

\begin{theorem}[Conditional modular moments]
\label{thm:moment3sum}
Fix an integer $k\ge1$. With the data structure of
Theorem~\ref{thm:refined}, choose $D=\Theta(n^e)$,
$s=\Theta(n^b)$, with $0<b<e/2<1/2$ and $z=e-2b>0$. Three-set integer 3SUM has a deterministic
decision algorithm with running time
\[
\softO\left(n^{1+z/2}D^E+n^{2+z}D^{-G}
+n^{2+z/2}D^{q-1/2}+n^{2-kz}
+n^{1+(k+1/2)e+z/2}+n^{2-b}+n^{1+z/2}D\right),
\]
provided $n^{1+z/2}\ge D^T\operatorname{poly}(\log D)$.
The distinct-element one-set form has the same bound, up to
polylogarithmic factors. An actual witness can also be recovered with
polylogarithmic overhead.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Choose the bucket prime $r\in[R,2R]$, where $R=\lceil n/s\rceil$, and
partition into sub-buckets as in Theorem~\ref{thm:det3sum}. Thus
$\Phi(r)=O(ns\log n)$ and the number $Q_0$ of compatible sub-queries
$u=(w,\alpha,\beta)$ is $\softO(n^2/s)$.
Let $P=\Theta(D)$ with $2P\le D$, and let $\mathcal P$ contain all primes
in $[P,2P]$ except $r$. Let $U\ge2$ be an integer bounding every
$|x+y-w|$ in the input, and put $\lambda=\lfloor\log_2U\rfloor+1$.
Every nonzero difference has at most $\lambda$ distinct prime divisors.
These choices are computable from the input; no real-number comparison is
needed in the selection rule below. Small sizes with fewer than $k$
available primes may be handled directly.

For each sub-query, define its actual pair count
\[
c_p(u)=\#\{(x,y)\in\alpha\times\beta:x+y\equiv w\pmod p\}.
\]
This counts multiplicities; it need not equal the Boolean product entry
of Lemma~\ref{lem:bucket}, but it is positive exactly when that entry is
positive. For distinct primes $p_1,\ldots,p_t$, put
\[
J_t=\sum_u\prod_{j=1}^t c_{p_j}(u),\qquad J_0=Q_0.
\]

\emph{Exact objective computation.} Put $M_t=r\prod_{j=1}^t p_j$.
For each sub-bucket $\alpha$ of $A$, enumerate every ordered tuple
$(x_1,\ldots,x_t)\in\alpha^t$, allowing repeated coordinates, and add one
to a histogram at the unique CRT residue $v\bmod M_t$ satisfying
\[
v\equiv\operatorname{bucket}(\alpha)\pmod r,
\qquad v\equiv x_j\pmod{p_j}\quad(1\le j\le t).
\]
Build the analogous histogram for $B$. The exact cyclic convolution of
these two histograms, summed at $w\bmod M_t$ for $w\in C$, equals $J_t$.
Indeed, the congruence modulo $r$ imposes bucket compatibility, while the
other coordinates impose the $t$ modular equations independently. Expanding
the product of pair counts makes exactly these independent choices of
witnesses inside the same two sub-buckets. This is not a test that a
single witness passes all primes.

The number of tuples is at most $n s^{t-1}$ per side. CRT coefficients can
be precomputed, and $t\le k$ is fixed. Histogram construction therefore
costs $\softO(n s^{t-1}+M_t)$. Exact cyclic convolution costs
$\softO(M_t)$ using Kronecker substitution and integer multiplication, as
in the preceding reduction. Its coefficients count at most
$n^2s^{2t-2}$ tuple pairs, and the final objective is at most
$n^3s^{2t-2}$, so their bit lengths are $O_k(\log n)$.
Also $M_t=n^{O_k(1)}$; indices, residues, and arithmetic intermediates use
$O_k(\log n)$ bits, which require only a constant number of RAM words.
Thus the exact arithmetic does not introduce an uncharged power of $n$.

\emph{Greedy choice with an affirmative certificate.} Suppose
$p_1,\ldots,p_{t-1}$ are fixed. Exclude them from $\mathcal P$, and let
$m_t$ be the number of remaining candidates. Compute $J_t(p)$ for each
candidate $p$ and choose a minimizer. If
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:moment-yes}
m_t J_t(p)>\lambda s^2 J_{t-1}.
\end{equation}
return YES. Otherwise retain this prime and continue, for at most $k$ stages.
The comparison in~\eqref{eq:moment-yes} is an exact integer comparison.

To justify the affirmative answer, assume that the instance has no exact
solution. Every difference in every sub-query is then nonzero, so
\[
\sum_{p\text{ remaining}}c_p(u)
\le\lambda |\alpha||\beta|\le\lambda s^2.
\]
Multiply by the nonnegative weight
$\prod_{j<t}c_{p_j}(u)$ and sum over $u$. The average candidate objective
is at most $\lambda s^2 J_{t-1}/m_t$, so its minimum cannot satisfy~\eqref{eq:moment-yes}.
Hence~\eqref{eq:moment-yes} is a valid certificate that a solution exists. No knowledge of
the number of genuine witnesses is assumed.

If all $k$ stages complete, then $m_t=\Omega(P/\log P)$ and
$\lambda=O(\log n)$ give
\[
J_k\le Q_0\prod_{t=1}^k\frac{\lambda s^2}{m_t}
=\softO\left(\frac{n^2}s\left(\frac{s^2}P\right)^k\right).
\]
Build the $k$ thin products of Lemma~\ref{lem:bucket} and query every
compatible sub-query in each product. Scan a sub-query only if every
answer is positive. Each such sub-query contributes a positive integer
to $J_k$, so there are at most $J_k$ scans. Each scan costs
$O(s\log n)$, and their total cost is
$\softO(n^2(s^2/P)^k)=\softO(n^{2-kz})$.
Every exact solution passes all filters, and every scanned answer is
checked in the original integers. Thus the procedure is correct whether
it returns through~\eqref{eq:moment-yes} or through scanning. If $J_t=0$, it may return NO
immediately, since a genuine solution would contribute at least one.

\emph{Complete cost.} Stage $t$ tries $O(P)$ candidates while the earlier
primes are fixed. Its cost is
\[
\softO\left(Pn s^{t-1}+rP^{t+1}\right).
\]
Since $s\le P$ and $r=\Theta(n/s)$, the second term dominates the first.
For fixed $k$ all stages therefore cost
$\softO((n/s)P^{k+1})=
\softO(n^{1+(k+1/2)e+z/2})$.
The other costs are exactly the $k=O(1)$ copies of the folded products
and queries in Theorem~\ref{thm:det3sum}, together with bucket selection.
Enumeration of the sub-queries and sorting are covered by those terms.

\emph{Distinct elements and witnesses.} In the one-set form, forbidden
repeated-element zero triples would invalidate the NO-instance premise
used above. Instead use a deterministic separating family. Give the
elements distinct binary indices. Any three distinct indices have three
distinct patterns on some two bit positions: choose a bit separating one
index from the other two, then a bit separating the latter pair.
Enumerate the $O(\log^2n)$ pairs of positions and all ordered selections
of three distinct patterns among their four patterns. Use the corresponding
three disjoint classes for $A,B,-C$. Every legal triple occurs in one such
instance, and no instance permits a repeated element. If an actual
witness is wanted, repeatedly bisect one set and invoke this decision
algorithm on the restricted instance known to contain a solution. There
are $O(\log n)$ such calls, after which the three singletons give a witness.
These overheads are polylogarithmic.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Improved deterministic integer 3SUM]
Take
\[
(k,c,\theta,e,z)=(18,\ 22.4828,\ 0.111021,\ 0.0538428,\ 0.00021113).
\]
For the boxes, queries, scans, encodings, and greedy selection, the savings
below exponent $2$ are, respectively,
\[
\begin{split}
eG-z&=0.003800286893920\ldots,\\
e(1/2-q)-z/2&=0.003800294398088\ldots,\\
kz&=0.00380034,\\
1-eE-z/2&=0.003806152261295\ldots,\\
1-(k+1/2)e-z/2&=0.003802635.
\end{split}
\]
The other costs and tile condition have strict slack, as certified by
exact rational intervals. Consequently integer 3SUM, including its
distinct-element one-set form and witness recovery, has deterministic time
$O(n^{1.996200})$. The strict gap absorbs the polylogarithmic factors of
the convolutions, the separating family, and the decision self-reduction.
\end{corollary}
This new affirmative certificate is a decision argument. It does not by
itself give an all-edges Exact Triangle algorithm or improve the stated
deterministic APSP exponent. It does, however, apply to Exact Triangle
itself.

\subsection*{Deterministic decision Exact Triangle by conditional modular moments}
For a pair/block $u=(a,b,B)$ and a prime $p$ write
$N_p(u)=\#\{c\in B:\ p\mid S(a,b,c)\}$, the modular-witness count returned by
the folded product of Lemma~\ref{lem:fold}. The objective
$J_t=\sum_u\prod_{j\le t}N_{p_j}(u)$ counts tuples of witnesses, one per
prime, inside the same pair/block, and plays the role of the 3SUM objective.

\begin{theorem}[Conditional modular moments for Exact Triangle]
\label{thm:moment-et}
Fix an integer $k\ge1$ and rationals $\alpha>0$, $\mu>0$. Let the tripartite
graph have two outer parts of $R=n^\alpha$ vertices and a middle part of
$H=n^{\alpha\mu}$ vertices, with integer weights of magnitude $n^{O(1)}$.
With the data structure of Theorem~\ref{thm:refined} put $D=\Theta(n^{\alpha e})$,
$P=\Theta(n^{\alpha a})$, $h=\lfloor D/(2P)\rfloor=\Theta(n^{\alpha b})$,
$e=a+b$, $z=a-b>0$, and $b<\mu$. Exact Triangle (decision) has a deterministic
algorithm with running time $\softO(n^{\alpha(2+\mu)-\alpha\delta})$ for every
$\delta$ with
\begin{gather*}
eG-z>\delta,\qquad e(1/2-q)-z/2>\delta,\qquad kz>\delta,\qquad
1-e(E-1/4)-3z/4>\delta,\\
1-3e/4-3z/4>\delta,\qquad 1-a>\delta,\qquad 1+a/2>eT,
\end{gather*}
together with the selection condition
\[
0.628\,\mu-0.372\,(k-1)b-(k+1)a>\delta\ \text{ if }\mu+(k-1)b\le1,
\qquad
0.628-(k-1)b-(k+1)a>\delta\ \text{ otherwise},
\]
where $0.628$ and $0.372$ stand for $3-\omega$ and $\omega-2$ with the
source's bound $\omega<2.372$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Partition the middle part into $\lceil H/h\rceil$ blocks of at most $h$
vertices; there are $Q_0=R^2\lceil H/h\rceil$ pair/blocks. Let $\mathcal P$
be the primes in $[P,2P]$, $m_t$ the number still available at stage $t$,
and $\lambda=\lfloor\log_2(3n^{O(1)})\rfloor+1$ a bound on the number of
distinct prime divisors of any nonzero sum $S(a,b,c)$.

\emph{Exact objective.} For fixed $p_1,\ldots,p_{t-1}$ and a candidate $p$,
$J_t(p)=\sum_u\prod_{j<t}N_{p_j}(u)\,N_p(u)$ is the number of tuples
$(a,b,c_1,\ldots,c_t)$ with all $c_j$ in one block and $p_j\mid S(a,b,c_j)$
for every $j$ (with $p_t=p$). Over the ring
$\mathcal R_t=\mathbb Z[x_1,\ldots,x_t]/(x_1^{p_1}-1,\ldots,x_t^{p_t}-1)$,
let $\Pi[a,(B,c_1,\ldots,c_t)]=\prod_jx_j^{w(a,c_j)}$ and
$\Sigma[(B,c_1,\ldots,c_t),b]=\prod_jx_j^{w(c_j,b)}$, indexed by tuples inside
a block; then $J_t(p)$ is the sum over $(a,b)$ of the coefficient of
$\prod_jx_j^{-w(a,b)\bmod p_j}$ in $(\Pi\Sigma)[a,b]$, exactly as in the
source's Theorem 17 for one prime. The middle dimension is
$M_t=\lceil H/h\rceil h^t=\widetilde\Theta(Hh^{t-1})$, a ring multiplication
costs $\softO(\prod_jp_j)$ by Kronecker substitution to one integer product,
and the matrix product is blocked into square products of order $\min(R,M_t)$:
$R^2M_t^{\omega-2}$ ring operations when $M_t\le R$ and $M_tR^{\omega-1}$
otherwise. With $O(P)$ candidates, stage $t$ costs
$\softO(P^{t+1}R^2M_t^{\omega-2})$ or $\softO(P^{t+1}M_tR^{\omega-1})$,
dominated by $t=k$; in exponents of $n^\alpha$ this is
$2+\mu+0.372(\mu+(k-1)b)+(k+1)a-\mu$ in the first regime and
$\mu+(k-1)b+1.372+(k+1)a$ in the second, giving the two selection
conditions against the budget $2+\mu$. Coefficients count at most
$R^2H^kh^{k-1}$ tuples and all moduli are $n^{O(1)}$, so every integer has
$O_k(\log n)$ bits.

\emph{Greedy choice and certificate.} At stage $t$ choose a minimizer
$p_t$ of $J_t$. If the instance has no zero triangle, every $u$ has
$\sum_{p\in\mathcal P\text{ remaining}}N_p(u)\le\lambda h$, so summing with
the nonnegative weights $\prod_{j<t}N_{p_j}(u)$ gives
$\sum_pJ_t(p)\le\lambda hJ_{t-1}$ and $m_tJ_t(p_t)\le\lambda hJ_{t-1}$.
If instead $m_tJ_t(p_t)>\lambda hJ_{t-1}$, return YES; this is a valid
certificate. If $J_t=0$, return NO. Otherwise, after $k$ stages,
$J_k\le Q_0\prod_t(\lambda h/m_t)=\widetilde O(Q_0(h/P)^k)$.

\emph{Filtering.} For each chosen prime and block build the folded instance
of Lemma~\ref{lem:fold}, with outer dimension $R\lceil\sqrt p\rceil$ and
inner dimension at most $2Ph\le D$; the tile condition is
$R\sqrt P\ge\sqrt M$, that is, $1+a/2>eT$. Query every pair once per prime
and block, scan a pair/block with the original integer sums only if all $k$
answers are positive, and stop at the first zero. A genuine zero triangle
survives every filter, so the output is correct. Each scanned pair/block
contributes at least one to $J_k$, so the scans cost
$O(hJ_k)=\widetilde O(R^2H(h/P)^k)=\softO(n^{\alpha(2+\mu)-\alpha kz})$.

\emph{Remaining costs.} The $k$ data structures per block cost
\[
\softO\Big(\frac Hh(R\sqrt P)^2D^{-G}\Big),\quad
\softO\Big(\frac HhR\sqrt P\,D^E\Big),\quad
\softO\Big(\frac HhR^2D^q\Big),\quad
\softO\Big(\frac HhR\sqrt P\,D\Big)
\]
for boxes, encodings, queries, and writing the matrices; computing the
residues $w(a,b)\bmod p$ costs $O(kR^2)$. In exponents of $n^\alpha$ these give the stated savings
$eG-z$, $1-e(E-1/4)-3z/4$, $e(1/2-q)-z/2$, $1-3e/4-3z/4$, and $1-a$, as
in Theorem~\ref{thm:det3sum}. The condition $b<\mu$ ensures that blocks fit
in the middle part.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Deterministic Exact Triangle]
Take $\alpha=\mu=1$, $k=11$, and
\[
(c,\theta,e,z)=(21.62,\;0.11216,\;0.05671,\;0.00034).
\]
The savings for boxes, queries, failed scans, encodings, and selection
(second regime) are
\[
0.003743746\ldots,\quad 0.003744811\ldots,\quad 0.00374,\quad
0.004385942\ldots,\quad 0.00385,
\]
with the other terms and the tile condition far from binding; all exceed
$0.003739$. Integer Exact Triangle is
therefore decided deterministically in $O(n^{2.996261})$ time, and a zero
triangle is found by $O(\log n)$ decision calls on halves of one part, also
in $O(n^{2.996261})$ time. The source's Theorem 19 gives
$O(n^{3-0.00175}\log n)\le O(n^{2.9983})$, and the single-prime deterministic
all-edges bound above gives $O(n^{2.997875})$.
\end{corollary}
Numerical exploration found savings near $0.003746$ at $k=11$ in this
restricted family; this is not a proved supremum. Selection costs grow
with $P^{k+1}$. The aggregate
certificate decides Exact Triangle only. Applying the same threshold to each
outer pair separately localizes zeros to pairs at no extra cost.

\begin{theorem}[Per-pair conditional moments]
\label{thm:per-pair-moments}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:moment-et}, all-edges Exact
Triangle, which asks for every outer pair $(a,b)$ whether some middle
vertex $c$ has $S(a,b,c)=0$, has a deterministic algorithm with the same
running time $\softO(n^{\alpha(2+\mu)-\alpha\delta})$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $\pi=(a,b)$ for an outer pair, $Z(\pi,B)=\#\{c\in B:\ S(a,b,c)=0\}$,
$\rho_t=\lambda h/m_t$, and
\[
J_t(\pi;p)=\sum_B\prod_{j<t}N_{p_j}(\pi,B)\,N_p(\pi,B),\qquad
J_{t-1}(\pi)=J_{t-1}(\pi;p_{t-1}),\qquad J_0(\pi)=\lceil H/h\rceil,
\]
so that $J_t(p)=\sum_\pi J_t(\pi;p)$. The product in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:moment-et} extracts the coefficient $J_t(\pi;p)$ of every
pair separately before summing, for every remaining candidate $p$, so the
whole table $(J_t(\pi;p))_{\pi,p}$ is available at the stated selection
cost.

Maintain a set $U_t$ of undecided pairs, with $U_0$ all $R^2$ pairs. At
stage $t$ declare YES every $\pi\in U_{t-1}$ with
\[
\min_{p}J_t(\pi;p)>\rho_t\,J_{t-1}(\pi),
\]
let $U_t$ be the other pairs of $U_{t-1}$, and choose $p_t$ minimizing
$\sum_{\pi\in U_t}J_t(\pi;p)$ over the remaining candidates.

\emph{Soundness.} If $Z(\pi,B)=0$ for every block, then
$\sum_pN_p(\pi,B)\le\lambda h$ for every block, so
$\sum_pJ_t(\pi;p)\le\lambda hJ_{t-1}(\pi)$ and
$\min_pJ_t(\pi;p)\le\rho_tJ_{t-1}(\pi)$. Every declared pair therefore has
a zero triangle.

\emph{Leak control.} Put
$\Gamma_{t-1}(\pi)=\sum_BZ(\pi,B)\prod_{j<t}N_{p_j}(\pi,B)$. Since
$N_p(\pi,B)\ge Z(\pi,B)$ term by term, $\Gamma_{t-1}(\pi)\le J_t(\pi;p)$
for every candidate $p$; hence $\Gamma_{t-1}(\pi)\le\rho_tJ_{t-1}(\pi)$ for
every $\pi\in U_t$.

\emph{Contraction.} A zero is divisible by all $m_t$ candidates and a nonzero
sum by at most $\lambda$ of them, so
$\sum_pN_p(\pi,B)\le m_tZ(\pi,B)+\lambda h$, and for $\pi\in U_t$
\[
\frac1{m_t}\sum_pJ_t(\pi;p)\le\Gamma_{t-1}(\pi)+\rho_tJ_{t-1}(\pi)
\le2\rho_tJ_{t-1}(\pi).
\]
The minimizer is at most the candidate average, so
$\sum_{\pi\in U_t}J_t(\pi;p_t)\le2\rho_t\sum_{\pi\in U_{t-1}}J_{t-1}(\pi)$,
and after $k$ stages
$\sum_{\pi\in U_k}J_k(\pi)\le2^kQ_0\prod_t\rho_t=\widetilde O(Q_0(h/P)^k)$
for fixed $k$.

\emph{Filtering.} Build the $k$ folded data structures per block as before,
query every pair/block with $\pi\in U_k$, scan a pair/block with the
original sums only if all $k$ counts are positive, and stop at the first
zero of each pair. A pair of $U_k$ whose scans find no zero is NO, since a
genuine zero passes every filter; so all $R^2$ answers are exact. Each
scanned pair/block contributes at least one to $\sum_{\pi\in U_k}J_k(\pi)$,
so the scans cost $O(h\,2^kQ_0(h/P)^k\operatorname{polylog})
=\softO(n^{\alpha(2+\mu)-\alpha kz})$; the constant $2^k$ is absorbed by
the strict gap $kz>\delta$. No other cost changes.
\end{proof}
The decision theorem certifies the aggregate; the per-pair thresholds use
the same table and the same $\lambda$, $h$, and $m_t$, and the uncertified
mass contracts by $2\rho_t$ instead of $\rho_t$.

\begin{corollary}[Deterministic all-edges Exact Triangle, min-plus product, and APSP]
\label{cor:det-all-edges-moments}
With $\alpha=\mu=1$ and the $k=11$ witness of the preceding corollary,
integer all-edges Exact Triangle takes $O(n^{2.996261})$ deterministic
time. The bit-scaling transfer of the subsection on integer APSP makes two
all-edges calls per bit level and $O(\log n)$ min-plus squarings, all
deterministic, and the strict gap $0.00374>0.003739$ absorbs the
logarithmic factors. Hence integer min-plus product and APSP take
$O(n^{2.996261})$ deterministic time, improving $O(n^{2.997875})$. For the
lopsided splits of Corollary~\ref{cor:det-clique-decision} below the same
transfer
computes the rectangular min-plus product, so Min- and Max-Weight
$k$-Clique with polynomially bounded integer weights take
$O(n^{k-0.003739\lfloor k/3\rfloor})$ deterministic time for every fixed
$k\ge3$, and $O(n^{4.99276})$ for $k=5$ through the split $(2,2,1)$.
\end{corollary}

\subsection*{Sharper deterministic square products}
\begin{lemma}[Rectangular conditional-moment selection]
\label{lem:rectangular-moment-selection}
Let $w(\kappa)$ be a certified upper bound on
$\omega(1,\kappa,1)$ over $\mathbb Q$. In the square outer-parts case of
Theorem~\ref{thm:per-pair-moments}, the selection condition can be replaced by
\[
3-w(1+(k-1)b)-(k+1)a>\delta.
\]
Here an arbitrarily small fixed exponent allowance for matrix multiplication
must fit within the displayed strict gap. Neither the per-pair certificate
nor its contraction changes.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
At stage $t$, a candidate prime requires multiplication of an
$n\times M_t$ matrix by an $M_t\times n$ matrix over the existing cyclic
polynomial ring, where $M_t=\widetilde O(nh^{t-1})$. Pad every middle
dimension to $\widetilde O(nh^{k-1})$. There are $O(P)$ candidates, and each
ring operation costs $\widetilde O(P^k)$ bit operations. Thus all fixed
$k$ stages cost
\[
\widetilde O\bigl(P^{k+1}n^{w(1+(k-1)b)+\varepsilon}\bigr).
\]
The monomial-matrix construction is covered by this bound since the matrix
multiplication exponent covers reading both inputs. Coefficient extraction
and all per-pair comparisons are covered as well.

For completeness the field algorithm can be used over this ring without
assuming divisions by ring elements. Fix an exact bilinear matrix
multiplication algorithm over $\mathbb Q$ at a sufficiently large but
constant base size to realize the chosen exponent allowance, then recurse.
Its only divisions are by its fixed nonzero rational constants, which are
invertible in the rational cyclic-polynomial algebra. Clearing those fixed
denominators level by level gives common denominators of size
$n^{O(1)}$. The linear encodings and recombinations at each of $O(\log n)$
levels amplify coefficient magnitudes by only a fixed constant per level;
the bilinear multiplication adds at most a polynomial number of products.
Consequently intermediate numerators and denominators have $O(\log n)$
bits for fixed $k$ and fixed base algorithm. The exact Kronecker
substitution bound for a ring multiplication therefore remains
$\widetilde O(P^k)$, including bit complexity. The final extracted
coefficients are the same nonnegative integers as in the original selector.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Published rectangular bound and interpolation.}
Table 1 of Vassilevska Williams, Xu, Xu, and Zhou,
\emph{New Bounds for Matrix Multiplication: from Alpha to Omega}
(SODA 2024; \href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.07970v2}{arXiv:2307.07970v2}),
gives
\[
\omega(1,1.2,1)\le2.535063,\qquad
\omega(1,1.5,1)\le2.794941.
\]
These are external matrix-multiplication bounds. Our local rational
certificate checks their subsequent use, not their source tensor analysis.
For $1.2\le\kappa\le1.5$, tensor products of the two algorithms give
\[
w(\kappa)=2.535063+
\frac{2.794941-2.535063}{1.5-1.2}(\kappa-1.2)
=2.535063+0.86626(\kappa-1.2).
\]
Indeed, tensoring rational proportions $1-\lambda$ and $\lambda$ of fixed
base algorithms gives outer dimensions with exponent one and middle
exponent $(1-\lambda)1.2+\lambda1.5$, while the operation exponent is the
same convex combination. Rational proportions suffice for our rational
$\kappa$; fixing the base large enough absorbs each algorithm's exponent
allowance. This is an upper bound by interpolation, not interpolation of
unproved numerical optima.


\begin{theorem}[Batched moments with deterministic lazy encodings]
\label{thm:det-apsp-refined}
Let $k\ge1$ be fixed, $c>10$, $0<r<\alpha<\theta$, $r<1-\theta$,
and $\theta/(1-r)<6/7$. Here $\alpha$ is the encoding-pruning fraction,
not the outer-size parameter of Theorem~\ref{thm:moment-et}; both outer
parts now have size $n$, as does the middle part. Put
\[
q_* = \max(q_1,q_2,q_{\rm lazy}),\quad
A_*=A_0(c)-G(c,\alpha),\quad
0<z<e,\quad a=(e+z)/2,\quad b=(e-z)/2<1.
\]
Let $w$ be a valid rectangular multiplication bound as in
Lemma~\ref{lem:rectangular-moment-selection}. Integer all-edges Exact
Triangle, min-plus product, and APSP have deterministic time
$O(n^{3-\delta})$ whenever, for some fixed $\varepsilon>0$,
\begin{gather*}
eG(c,\theta)-z>\delta,\qquad e(1/2-q_*)-z/2>\delta,\qquad kz>\delta,\\
1-e(A_*-1/4)-3z/4>\delta,\qquad
3-w(1+(k-1)b)-(k+1)a-\varepsilon>\delta,\\
1-3e/4-3z/4>\delta,\qquad 1-a>\delta,\\
1+a/2>eA_0(c),\qquad 1-b>eC_K(c).
\end{gather*}
APSP retains the no-negative-cycle assumption and integer weights have
polynomial magnitude.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Run the per-pair certificates and common-prime minimization of
Theorem~\ref{thm:per-pair-moments}. Lemma~\ref{lem:rectangular-moment-selection}
bounds the selection work by the fifth inequality. All $k$ primes are
chosen deterministically, and the final scans cost
$\widetilde O(n^{3-kz})$ by the same $2\rho_t$ contraction.

For each chosen prime $p\in[P,2P]$, residue folding gives
$B=\lceil n/h\rceil=\Theta(n^{1-b})$ products, each with outer dimension
$N=n\lceil\sqrt p\rceil=\Theta(n^{1+a/2})$ and common inner dimension
$D=\Theta(n^e)$, padded to a power of four. Pad missing matrix entries
with zero indicators. Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:direct-sum} to these $B$
products, with the deterministic representation of Lemma~\ref{lem:cyclic-lazy}.
The final two inequalities ensure $N\ge N_0$ and $B\ge K$, including
rounding. Reducing the encoding budget does not reduce $N_0$ or $K$.
The encodings, boxes, and all prefix-contracted queries cost, respectively,
\[
\widetilde O(BND^{A_*}),\qquad
\widetilde O(BN^2D^{-G(c,\theta)}),\qquad
\widetilde O(Bn^2D^{q_*}).
\]
Their exponents are $2-b+a/2+eA_*$, $3-b+a-eG(c,\theta)$, and
$3-b+eq_*$. Subtracting from $3$ gives the asserted savings.
